In answer to .our letter of August 21, 1942 with 
respect to estimates of a residual figure of Japanese firstline 
air strength, sour attention is invited to the fact that the 
surcticon of preparing estimates of enemy air strength has been 
made a responsibility of the G-2 Division of the War Department 
veneval start. There is a considerable Air Force representation 
of officers :n that division. All data with respect to enemy 
air losses are gathered by the G<2 Division. Estimates of the 
losses of airplanes due to all causes other than those knowm 
“3 hava oceen destroyed in the air are also made by that division, 


Considerable confusion has developed in high places 
at various times as a result of several agencies evaluating 
Sasic data and presenting estimates which are not in agreement. 
vor that principal reason we do not attempt, in this office, 
to arrive at an evaluation of enemy air strength but depend 
upon that preduced by the G2 Division to answer our purposes, 


It is racommended that your office follow the same 
procedure Sy securiog information wf enemy air strength fcrom 
She G2 Division of the veneral Staff rather than to attempt 
an independent evaluation based upon rules and factors which. 
aust, of necessity, be anmpirical in nature. It is believed 
that opti is means we will avoid the posgsitility of Jiverze 
astinatee reachilag higher authority, and of possibly causing 
ConP Us otte 


Sincerely yours, 


ay 


H. H. ARNOLD, \ 
Lientenant General, U.S.A... 
Commanding Gonornl, Amiy Air Foroas,

Burry Brophy 2 May 22, 1942 


In order to prevent the immediate location by direction-finding 
equipment of the subrarines' positions, it is recommended that one submarine 
surface off the coast south of Cape Inubo or alse in the Sea of Kasima Nada; 
that another submarina surface off the coast of the Sea of Kuwano Nada, both 
of which are en the eastern side of Japan; and that another submarine surface~- 
unless the hagards are tde great--off the western coast of Japan somewhere 
in the Japan Sea. hach submarine would have a duplicate set of transcriptions. 
Tiguratively speaking, the ball could be passed from the submarine in the 
east ta the submarine in the west and from the submarine in the west to the 
submarina in the southeast either at pre-arranged invervals or else at the 
fifteen ainute break. This ought to confuse Japanese aviation and Japanese 
racic technicians, teonuse the Amerivan signals would first come from the 
northeast, than from the west, and then from the southeast. Even if the 
Heme Defense awakened in time to make a serious attempt to locate the source 
of the program, they would be unable to accurately spot such locations. 

ings mone of the submarines would be broadcasting more than fifteen minutes 
aX any ene time, the chances appear good that each submarine in turn could 
complete ite aisston and slip away without being spotted. 


& program of this kind might concelvabiy cause chacs and confusion 
amt agtesl deaths among the population equivalent to an actual landing by a 
stall nusber of troops, It should achieve, first, actual damage caused by 
she panic of the posilation; second, deteriorstion of morale inside Japan; 
third, sstablishment of a state of nerves on the part of the militarists; 
fourth, loas of prestige of the armed forces of Japan in the eyes of the 
people; and fifth, loss of face by Japan in the eyes of the word--parti- 
cularty Gareany and Italy. It might establish a precedent which could be 
effectively repeated as a weapon for a propaganda ‘war of nerves,"

When you telephoned just now I am afraid I did not express what 
{ meant about probabilities of Japanese attack. I now try to. 


The Alaska~Japan situation cannot be divorced from that of Siberia- 
vapan, particularly not in the Japanese mind; for the Sovlets may join us 
im counter-attack. 


My wiew has been that if the Sovieta chose the time for a war with 
dagan they would start in Ootober; that if the Japanese chose the time 
they would start in May. My reasons for thia follow: 


The Japaness, I belipve, understand cold weather conditions of 
warfare less wall than any other great power; correapondingly they probably 
over-watinate the superiority of the Soviet Army in winter equipment and 
winter methods, sa dompared with those of summer. Therefore they almost 
neaecsarily attack in the early spring, so as to have ahead of them the 
longest possible apan of that weather to which they believe their own 
equd peant and methods best buited in a war that will cover the Amur front 
ami the Siberian coastline ‘bowards Bering Strait. 


{? facing a situation where they believed the Sovieta would come 
to our ald, the dapanioas would nove just as soon as they considered the 
ioe conditions favorable ina the Okhotwk Sea and up along northeastward. 
Shia would be lete May or aurly June. They would start with an attempt 
te knoek out Duteh Harbor, wince thia is our most effective bare for naval 
ask alz cooperation with th» Sovieta; then they would try to knock out the 
fovtat tastallatians at Froridonee Bay, Anadyr, KMarkovo, Komandorski, Petro- 
gevlavek, amd the Okhotek Sua, Magaevo, Komsomolek, Viadivostok. Their plan 
@euid be Gy win on the described front, and on the Manchurian front, before 
the coming af those northesst Siberian winter donditiona which they dread 
ont which start in October. 


if the Japanade were sure that the Soviets would not join in the war 
thes [ see ao reason way Hey wight net attack Alaska at any season, What 
they dread iO: the fieviet alan fe a power which comes at them from the 
tateriar. Pram the scrth.
